L1970: A variety of vascular or inflammatory findings were observed due to intravenous infusion of neoGAA via the implanted femoral catheter and vascular access port (venous access port and/or right/left saphenous veins). These findings included thrombi in the lung, accompanied by inflammation and an occasional fragment of foreign material (catheter), and thrombi, fibrosis, inflammation, and/or hemorrhage in the vein proximal to catheter tip and in the saphenous veins. These findings occurred at comparable incidences in vehicle and test article-treated monkeys. Thus, there were no neoGAA treatment-related histopathologic changes.
L1971: Anti-neoGAA antibody titers were detected in all neoGAA-treated animals prior to the 7th infusion. Anti-neoGAA antibody titers were increased in the majority of neoGAA-treated animals prior to the 13th infusion when compared to titers at the 7th infusion.
L1972: Following the first, seventh, and thirteenth dose, systemic exposure increased in a greater than dose proportional manner. AUC and Cmax values from the 7th and 13th infusions were increased relative to those from the first infusion. These time-dependent changes were associated with a statistically significant decrease in clearance. Half-life was increased and clearance was decreased with increased dose level. These results suggest that the major plasma clearance mechanism, presumably uptake in tissues by CIM6Pr, was saturated at the tested dose levels. Furthermore, the change in TK parameters after repeated dosing may have been related to the presence of anti-drug antibodies. Overall, there was no gender-related difference in TK parameters. L1978: In summary, cynomolgus monkeys were treated with neoGAA at doses of 50 or 200 mg/kg every two weeks via an intravenous infusion for 26-weeks. There were no treatment-related changes in clinical signs, ophthalmic examination, ECG, neurobehavioral parameters, respiratory rate, core temperature, hematology, coagulation, or clinical chemistry. Animals in the 200 mg/kg group showed slight decreases in body weight gain and food consumption during the treatment period. A dose-dependent decrease in some organ weights was observed. However, there were no neoGAA-related macroscopic or microscopic findings. The TK data suggested that saturation of plasma clearance had occurred. All animals treated with neoGAA were positive for anti-neoGAA antibodies. Target organs of toxicity were not identified. The NOAEL was 50 mg/kg, based on impaired weight gain in the 200 mg/kg group. However, 200 mg/kg is considered to be a tolerated dose, given that the observed effects were limited to small reductions in weight gain and organ weights.
L1979: ## Genotoxicity Studies
L1980: NeoGAA is produced by conjugating a synthetic glycan (Genz-669342) to oxidized sialic acid residues on rhGAA through a synthetic linker with an aminoxy moiety. The synthetic linker portion of Genz-669342 is structurally similar to hydroxylamine, which has the potential to be genotoxic. Thus, the Sponsor conducted *in vitro* and *in vivo* genotoxicity studies to evaluate the genotoxic potential of Genz-669342 and neoGAA.
L1981: Genz-669342 was negative in the Ames test and chromosomal aberration test in cultured human peripheral blood lymphocytes.
L1982: The genotoxic potential of neoGAA was evaluated in two mouse micronucleus assays in GAA knockout mice. Since neoGAA is a biotechnology-derived pharmaceutical, the methods used in these studies were not compliant with the recommendations in ICH guidance S2(R1), nor were the studies GLP-compliant.

Immediately following euthanasia, whole blood was collected and placed in fixative for micronuclei assessment. Micronuclei formation was determined using the MicroFlow® detection kit [REDACTED](b) (4) per the manufacturer's instructions.

The percentage of MN-RET and MN-NCE was comparable to the kit negative control.
L1989: ## (2). Repeat Dose Genotoxicity Study of neoGAA Administered Intravenously to GAA KO Mice (Genzyme Study 10-00184)
L1990: GAA knockout mice were administered vehicle or neoGAA intravenously at doses of 0 and 50 mg/kg on day 1, for a total of 3 doses over an 8 hour period (0, 4, and 8 hours) via lateral tail vein. The mice were euthanized on days 8 and 15. The study design is summarized in the Sponsor's table below. The Sponsor provided the following rationale for the study design:
L1991: **"NeoGAA may take greater than 24 hours to be metabolized following uptake into tissues and cells, and this metabolism may be necessary for the glycan/linker to be liberated *in vivo*. Therefore, to evaluate the potential of neoGAA to induce micronuclei formation over time, Genzyme Study 10-00184 was conducted to evaluate micronuclei formation 7 and 14 days following administration of neoGAA."**
L1992: Following euthanasia, blood was collected for micronucleus analysis using the MicroFlow® assay.
L1993: **Table 12: Study Design for Repeat Dose Micronucleus Assay with neoGAA Administered Intravenously to GAA KO Mice**

L1996: Under the conditions of this study, neoGAA at doses of 50 mg/kg/dose, administered at 0, 4, and 8 hr on day 1, did not increase the number of MN-RET or MN-NCE at 7 or 14 days following the day of treatment, compared to the vehicle group and the negative control provided with the kit.
L1997: ## SUMMARY AND EVALUATION:
L1998: In order to support the proposed Phase 1 clinical study design, nonclinical toxicity studies for a duration of 6 months in a rodent and nonrodent species are needed for safety evaluation. The Sponsor conducted short-term (up to 1 month duration) general toxicology studies in mice and monkeys, and the toxicological findings from these studies are similar. Therefore, a 6-month toxicity study in one species is considered sufficient for safety assessment.
L1999: In the 26-week intravenous toxicity study in cynomolgus monkeys (18 months of age) with a 4-week recovery period, neoGAA was administered at doses of 50 and 200 mg/kg, every other week. NeoGAA had no effects on clinical signs, ophthalmic examination, ECG, neurobehavioral parameters, respiratory rate, core temperature, hematology, clinical chemistry, or histopathology. Animals in the 200 mg/kg group showed slight body weight reduction, decreased body weight gain, and decreased food consumption during the treatment period. A
L2000: dose-dependent decrease in some organ weights was observed. No target organ of toxicity was identified. Anti-neoGAA antibodies were detected in all animals treated with neoGAA. The NOAEL was 50 mg/kg, based on impaired weight gain in the 200 mg/kg group. However, 200 mg/kg (2400 \text{ mg/m}^2) is considered to be a tolerated dose, given that the observed effects were limited to small reductions in weight gain and organ weights. Based on a body surface area comparison, the tolerated dose provides a 13-fold safety margin for the starting dose of 5 mg/kg (185 \text{ mg/m}^2 given every other week) in the proposed clinical study, and a 3.2-fold safety margin for the proposed maximum dose of 20 mg/kg (740 \text{ mg/m}^2). Therefore, the chronic toxicity study in monkeys provides a reasonable assurance of safety for the proposed clinical study.
L2001: NeoGAA is produced by conjugating a synthetic glycan (Genz-669342) to oxidized sialic acid residues on rhGAA through a synthetic linker with an aminoxy moiety. The synthetic linker portion of Genz-669342 is structurally similar to hydroxylamine, which has the potential to be genotoxic. However, Genz-669342 was negative in the Ames test and chromosomal aberration assay.
L2002: The Sponsor measured levels of unconjugated Genz-669342 in two clinical batches, and in four nonclinical batches that were used in the 26-week toxicity study in monkeys. The levels of unconjugated Genz-669342 in the current two clinical batches are lower or slightly higher than the qualification limit stated in ICH Q3B(R2) (0.2%, equivalent to 2.4 mg unconjugated Genz-669342 at the maximum neoGAA dose of 20 mg/kg, assuming a 60-kg bodyweight). In addition, the 26-week IV toxicity study in monkeys provided a 5.2-fold safety margin, based on body surface area, for the current levels of unconjugated Genz-669342 in clinical batches (up to (b)(4) \mu\text{g/ml}; see table below). This safety margin provides a reasonable assurance of safety for unconjugated Genz-669342 in the current clinical batches.
L2003: However, the Sponsor proposed a specification limit of (b)(4) \mu\text{g/ml} for unconjugated Genz-669342 in the drug product. Based on this limit, the maximum potential daily dose of unconjugated Genz-669342 from neoGAA will be (b)(4) \text{ mg} (assuming a 60-kg body weight, and a dose of 20 mg/kg neoGAA), which will greatly exceed the recommended ICH limit (2.4 mg/day). Furthermore, the maximum potential daily exposure to unconjugated Genz-669342 in patients will be (b)(4)-fold higher, based on body surface area comparison, than the tolerated dose in the 26-week IV toxicity study in monkeys (see table below). Therefore, the 26-week monkey study cannot provide an assurance of safety for the proposed specification limit of (b)(4) \mu\text{g/ml} for unconjugated Genz-669342. Thus, the proposed limit is unacceptable without additional toxicity studies to allow for safety assessment.

L2007: A teleconference was held with the Sponsor on April 25, 2013 to discuss the safety concern related to the proposed limit for unconjugated Genz-669342 (free glycan). The Sponsor clarified that all batches (nonclinical and clinical) were manufactured using the same process. The concentration of free glycan in each batch is shown in the table below.
L2008: **Table 2: Residual Glycan Levels in Nonclinical and Current Clinical Trial Batches**
L2009: Lot numbers of Nonclinical batches	Lot numbers of Current clinical trial batches
NE006	NE007	NE008	NE009	12CT010	12CT011
Residual glycan level (\mug/ml)	(b) (4)
L2010: In light of the observed levels of free glycan in the neoGAA batches, the Agency recommended a limit of (b) (4) \mug/ml for this impurity. This limit was selected to provide assurance that free glycan levels in future clinical batches are likely to be compliant with the specification, while still providing a reasonable assurance of safety for patients. The maximum potential dose of free glycan will be (b) (4) \mug/m^2 in patients, at the limit of (b) (4) \mug/ml. The 26-week IV toxicity study in monkeys provides a (b) (4)-fold safety margin based on a \mug/m^2 comparison. This small margin is considered to be acceptable for assurance of safety, given that the tolerated dose was the highest dose tested (i.e. no major signs of toxicity were observed at any dose level). The Sponsor agreed to the Agency's recommended limit, and submitted a written response on April 26, 2013 (SD # 5) that reflects this agreement.
L2011: During the teleconference, the Agency also informed the Sponsor that an additional chronic toxicity study would be needed to support an increase in the limit for free glycan. In the amendment submitted on April 26, 2013, the Sponsor stated that they will likely contact the Agency to discuss the requirements and design for a chronic toxicity study to support qualification of free glycan above (b) (4) \mug/ml.
L2012: ## RECOMMENDATIONS:
L2013: - From a nonclinical viewpoint, the proposed clinical study is safe to proceed.
- In the absence of additional nonclinical data, the limit for free glycan (unconjugated Genz-669342) in the drug product should not be increased above the current limit of (b) (4) \mug/ml. If the Sponsor prefers to increase the limit for free glycan, the Agency recommends that the Sponsor submit a proposal for a chronic toxicity study, in addition to the proposed new limit for free glycan.

L2026: Figure 3 illustrates the Trial Design (PAP and ETP) for Trial EFC14028 (COMET).
The trial begins with a Screening period (D-14 to D-1). This leads into the Blinded Treatment Period, where patients receive Avalglucosidase alfa (n=48) or alglucosidase alfa (n=48) 20 mg/kg IV infusion qow for a total of 25 doses. This period spans from D1 to W49, with infusions occurring at D1, D6, W3, W5, W7, W9, W41, W43, W45, W47, and W49.
Following the Blinded Treatment Period, the trial proceeds to Primary Analysis after all patients complete W49.
Following Primary Analysis, the trial enters the Open-label avalglucosidase alfa Long Term Follow-up period, where patients receive Avalglucosidase alfa (n=96) 20 mg/kg IV infusion qow for a total of 48 doses. This period spans from W51 to W145, with infusions occurring at W51, W52, W53, W55, W57, W59, W61, W137, W139, W141, W143, and W145.
Following the Open-label Long Term Follow-up, the trial enters the Extended Open-label avalglucosidase alfa Long Term Follow-up period, where patients receive Avalglucosidase alfa (n=96) 20 mg/kg IV infusion qow for a total of up to 72 doses (or until avalglucosidase alfa is approved, whichever comes first). This period spans from W147 to W253, with infusions occurring at W147, W149, W151, W153, W155, W157, W283, W285, W287, W289, and W293, concluding at EOS (End of Study).
Legend:
W = Week
D = Day
I = Infusion
IV = Intravenous
EOS = End of study visit
QOW = every other week

L2040: Figure 18 is a graph showing FVC upright (% predicted) versus Follow-up duration (years) for patients treated with Alglucosidase Alfa. L2043: Schematic diagram showing the structure of Glycan E13 conjugated to Alglucosidase alfa (rhGAA). The glycan structure (Glycan E13) is shown on the left, composed of 2 bisM6P (red), 4 mannose (blue), and a linker (black). The linker connects to an oxidized sialic acid (orange) on the Alglucosidase alfa (rhGAA) protein (pink). The conjugation is achieved via an oxime bond formed between the aminoxy nitrogen of the glycan linker and the carbon at position 7 (C7) of the oxidized sialic acid of the Alglucosidase alfa protein.
